Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Preview  |  October 2026

### Page 7

TO P I C  2 | C L I MATE F I N AN C E
Climate finance delivery will be a central test for COP31
June’s Bonn preparatory talks reportedly made little progress and exposed persistent finance divisions, leaving COP31 to turn both the $300bn and $1.3tn-by-2035 goals into a more credible
delivery plan. This is a big ask and requires clearer public/private roles, consistent tracking mechanisms, more concessional finance, risk-sharing and guarantees that can help reduce the cost of

capital. New large-scale finance commitments appear unlikely, with the EU already stating it will not make new pledges.
7
Climate finance: needs, commitments and progress
The image below explains the various climate finance estimates and goals.
Total estimated climate investment need for
developing countries (excluding China)
(annual, by 2035)
$3.2 trillion
per year by 2035
~$1.9 trillion
Domestic finance (within developing countries)
$1.3 trillion

International climate finance (from abroad) –
aligned with the NCQG $1.3tn goal
Source: Independent High-Level Expert Group on Climate
Finance (IHLEG)
$1.3 trillion a year by 2035
COP31 focus: first implementation update on the Baku-Belem
roadmap to $1.3T. This total is inclusive of the $300bn NCQG target.
Context: COP29 set the New Collective Quantified Goal (NCQG),
calling on all actors to scale climate finance for developing countries
to at least $1.3tn a year by 2035. No country has an assigned share,

and private capital is expected to count.
At COP31: first implementation update on the Baku-Belem roadmap
to $1.3T, based on “measurable progress” against targets including:
• a global monitoring framework to track progress in climate finance
mobilization, identify bottlenecks, and highlight emerging solutions.
• clear pathways for public, private, domestic and international
finance;
• Expanding operability, comparability, and interoperability of

climate-finance data; and
• action on grants and concessional finance, debt and fiscal space,
private-finance costs, capacity and system reform.
Are the NCQG and IHLEG aligned? IHLEG’s $1.3tn external-finance
estimate (within the $3.2tn needed excl. China) does align with the
NCQG $1.3tn goal and underpins the Baku-to-Belém Roadmap.
However, they are built differently: political target vs needs-based
estimate.
$300 billion a year by 2035
COP31 focus: what counts and who contributes. This amount

contributes towards the $1.3tn NCQG target from all actors.
Context: Developed countries agreed to lead mobilisation of at least
$300bn a year by 2035, counting public finance and the private
finance it mobilises; no country has a fixed share.
At COP31: Focus turns to how to deliver. Developing countries are
pushing for the development of action plans to chart a pathway to
$300bn and for the Article 9 work programme to be included on the
formal agenda (with emphasis on Article 9.1 – which explicitly covers

developed country finance provision).
Adaptation finance
COP31 focus: embedding and tracking the tripling pledge
Context: COP30 called for adaptation finance to at least triple by
2035, yet this was relatively vague; it did not specify a base year or a
target figure (some have suggested $120bn, i.e. 3x the 2025 goal).
At COP31: Parties need to resolve disagreement on whether to
anchor the pledge in the Global Goal on Adaptation (GGA) and put a
finer point on the agreed Belém Adaptation Indicators that will track

it, after disagreement stalled the June Bonn talks.
Loss and Damage Fund
COP31 focus: guidance, replenishment and access
Why it matters: The Fund is the only dedicated channel for
irreversible climate impacts, yet it has no annual funding target and
resources remain far below demand.
At COP31: Focus will be on its long-term funding strategy, with the
first replenishment cycle set to begin in 2027. $342m made available
in the first funding window, which offers $5–20m grants with at least

half reserved for SIDS and LDCs; but the voluntary nature of
payments, disputes over who pays and sensitivity around
“compensation” make major new pledges in Antalya unlikely.
Adaptation finance: 2019 to 2035
(assumed target TBD)
$18.8bn
2019
~$40bn
2025 goal
$34.7bn
2024
~$120bn
Possible 2035 goal
Pledges and received funds (as at end-25)
~$820 million
pledged by 27 partners
~$440 million
received by the fund
Hurdles to these
targets
Grants vs loans

Developing countries want grants (especially for
adaptation), citing debt burdens and historical
responsibility; funders favour loans and blended
structures that include private capital.
Pressure on development banks
The US is pushing MDBs (e.g. the World Bank), to drop
their climate focus, according to the World Resources
Institute (WRI). Developing countries want MDB reform
to deliver more investment and faster disbursement.
Competing budget pressures

Donors are diverting aid to fund domestic priorities
such as defence. There is also the reality that climate is
now a lower priority for some governments.
Reliance on private capital
The IHLEG estimates ~½ the $1.3tn could come from
private sources. Private capital is looking for regulation,
insurance and more guarantees to reduce risk.
Sources: IHLEG (2024), OECD climate finance data, Fund for Responding to Loss and Damage, World Resources Institute (WRI), Baku to Belém Roadmap to 1.3T.

### Page 8

TO P I C  3 | AD AP TATI O N  &  R ES I L I EN C E (1  o f 2 )
Adaptation will be one of the most important, and more divisive, negotiating topics
Adaptation is also set to be on the formal negotiating agenda at COP31. UN warnings that an overshoot of the Paris Agreement’s 1.5°C goal is now unavoidable (at least temporarily), have
dampened morale for climate activists. As it relates to the COP agenda on adaptation, parties appear deadlocked on whether the COP30 goal to triple adaptation finance by 2035 should be

included in the Global Goal on Adaptation (GGA) text. This is important, as anchoring it to the GGA could signal to investors the scale and timing of future public and blended finance flows.
The role of adaptation in the COP process
The Global Goal on Adaptation (GGA) was established under the Paris Agreement to enhance
adaptive capacity, strengthen resilience and reduce vulnerability to climate change. At COP30
last year, countries adopted 59 indicators (still being refined) to measure progress against the

goal. They also agreed a goal to triple adaptation finance by 2035. The pledge was vague: it set
neither a concrete figure nor a baseline year (a potential annual target of $120bn would be
triple the previous goal of c.$40bn by 2025).
Why GGA talks were deadlocked at the June climate meetings
Finance was the main sticking point at the June Bonn meetings. Developing countries wanted
the COP30 goal to triple adaptation finance by 2035 referenced in the GGA text; countries

including Canada, Norway and Japan opposed. Parties also split on whether the indicators task
force should be expert- or party-led, so no GGA text was agreed and the decision passes to
COP31. Moving the Adaptation Fund to serve the Paris Agreement exclusively (funded by a 5%
share of proceeds from Article 6.4 credits) stalled in Bonn over the composition of its board;
talks will resume at COP31.
What is needed at COP31 to unlock adaptation progress

Adaptation is on the provisional COP31 agenda, including the tripling adaptation finance goal
(though this may get blocked). Some parties want it explicitly referenced in the final COP31
GGA decision; this should become clearer when the agenda is adopted as COP31 opens on 9
Nov. At issue: the tripling pledge still lacks a baseline and named contributors. At COP30,
developed nations resisted new finance obligations, while the EU and many others pushed for

a roadmap to transition away from fossil fuels, which a group of mostly oil- and gas-producing
countries blocked. The result was a looser tripling goal and no agreed fossil-fuel roadmap.
Developing countries are unlikely to give ground, as adaptation relies far more on public
funding than mitigation, so a repeat of COP30’s finance vs. mitigation debate is plausible.
Sources: United Nations, UNEP, Carbon Brief, European Parliament (COP30 outcomes briefing, December 2025)

### Page 9

TO P I C  3 | AD AP TATI O N  &  R ES I L I EN C E (2  o f 2 )
The EU’s proposed Climate Resilience Law plans for a 3°C world, while reiterating commitment to net zero 2050
The EU Commission’s recently proposed Climate Resilience Law would set a reference global warming trajectory of 3°C, providing a benchmark for member states’ adaptation planning. The EU
states it remains committed to reaching net zero by 2050, but some at COP will likely view the proposal as accepting overshoot. With the EU also pointing to an estimated need for EUR70bn in

annual adaptation investment, this provides an interesting backdrop as COP31 seeks to resume adaptation negotiations which stalled earlier this year, primarily due to finance.
What the Commission proposed
On 27 October the Commission presented its EU Framework for Climate Resilience, anchored
by a proposed European Climate Resilience Law. With current global policy commitments
pointing to around 2°C of warming by 2050 and 3°C by 2100, the law would set a common

reference trajectory of 1.5°C in 2030, 2°C in 2050 and 3°C in 2100, with this regarded as the
“baseline assumption”. Member states would be required to use it in national climate risk
assessments and adopt a single national resilience plan by end-2030.
Why it matters: the EU is planning for overshoot
The EU highlighted warnings that overshoot of the 1.5°C goal is now “unavoidable” (even if just
temporarily) as necessitating policy action, even as the EU says it is still committed to climate

neutrality by 2050. The Commission links the plan to Europe’s hottest summer on record and
estimates annual economic losses from climate extremes of €50bn over the past four years,
adding that output losses caused by climate change go far beyond this. It says EU adaptation
needs are ~€70bn a year through to 2050, with Europe warming at 2x the global rate.
What it means for COP31
The proposal demonstrates that warnings on the implications of a significant overshoot of the

Paris Agreement global warming limits are moving from scientific theory to real government
policy. While the opening line of the new EU framework emphasized it remains committed to
reaching net zero by 2050, some may view a 3°C planning baseline as accepting overshoot.
The headline €70bn annual EU adaptation finance requirement also provides an interesting
backdrop heading into Global Goal on Adaptation talks given they are stalled largely due to

disagreement over explicit links to finance in the GGA text. For context, the UNEP estimates
annual adaptation needs for developing countries of $310–365bn by 2035. Ultimately, we
expect adaptation focus to continue growing as warming impacts are increasingly felt and
societal calls for relief grow louder. Corporates will need to consider potential strategic
impacts, while some investors have already begun raising their baseline warming forecasts.

### Page 10

TO P I C  4 | EL EC TR I F I C ATI O N
Türkiye is championing a voluntary pledge to lift electrification from 23% to 35% by 2035
Electrification is expected to be at the centre of this “implementation COP”, targeting a rise in electricity’s share of global final energy use from 23% to 35% by 2035. We expect support for this
action given it is being positioned alongside energy security, but we also expect discussion and debate over how it is delivered (e.g., the role of renewables, nuclear and natural gas), alongside

concerns over clean tech dependency in supply chains. China and the EU have expressed support but not yet committed.
Some may brand this the electrification COP
The Action Agenda pledge aims to raise electricity's share of global final energy from 23%
today to 35% by 2035. The pledge was launched in Bonn this past June and formalised as the
"35-by-35 Global Electrification Pledge" in New York on 21 September.
China and the EU have signaled support, but have not yet signed the pledge

Electrification fits modern day energy-security goals making it a viable target for this year’s
COP. China’s cleantech dominance means it has a further incentive to support initiatives that
seek to increase the pace of global electrification. It also aligns closely with China's existing
development trajectory. While the 35% goal is based on Paris-aligned pathways supported by
IEA and IRENA analysis, the numerical target itself does not require the additional electricity to

come from non fossil fuel sources. This has invited some criticism, though it may make it
easier for some nations to sign the non-binding pledge.
Alignment with the voluntary renewables pledge
Recall that COP28 called for tripling global renewable power capacity by 2030 in COP28’s
formal Global Stocktake decision, equivalent to around 11.2 TW. IRENA’s September 2026
assessment puts capacity at 5.15 TW at end-2025. Record additions of 693 GW in 2025 remain

below the 1,200 GW needed annually in 2026–30. The 35-by-35 pledge complements this
supply goal by shifting transport, buildings and industry towards electricity, enabling
renewable power to replace fossil fuels. Its emissions benefit still depends on cleaner
generation.
We view the goal positively, but delivery needs grid enhancements.
IRENA counts ~2,500 GW of renewable, storage and large-load projects awaiting connection
and says annual investment to strengthen grid infrastructure and enhance system flexibility

must rise from ~$500bn in 2025 to around $1tn through 2035, and $1.2tn thereafter. That
said, signatories signal support, but domestic delivery plans will determine investment.
Sources: UNFCCC, COP31 Presidency, IEA, IRENA

TO P I C  5   |   F O S S I L  F U EL S
The fossil fuel transition roadmap track is growing outside the formal COP process
COP30 failed to agree a fossil fuel transition roadmap, despite support from more than 80 countries. Brazil’s COP30 presidency is therefore developing a roadmap outside formal UN
negotiations to be presented ahead of COP31. Separately, Colombia and the Netherlands convened a ‘coalition of the willing’ of 57 countries in Santa Marta in April 2026 to explore practical

steps towards a fossil fuel phase-out. This parallel forum allows willing countries to cooperate without needing consensus from all COP parties.
Sources: Carbon Brief, Santa Marta conference report

TO P I C  6 | TR AD E
Trade could resurface at COP31 as a challenge to collective agreement; talks have been requested
Trade tensions could feature at COP31 as governments weigh climate ambition against industrial competitiveness. Developing countries fear that measures such as the CBAM disadvantage

their exporters, while the EU says they prevent carbon leakage. COP30 agreed three annual dialogues on trade's role in climate cooperation (no binding outcome), creating for the first time a
formal space for trade discussions in the UNFCCC process, but The African Group and others would like it on the formal COP31 agenda.
Do trade discussions belong at COP?
Developing countries have long pushed back against climate-related trade measures such as
the EU's Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism (CBAM) and Deforestation Regulation (EUDR).

They argue such measures are protectionism disguised as climate policy and
disproportionately impact poorer countries. Developed countries view such measures as
necessary to level the playing field for domestic firms in cases where foreign competitors face
lower environment-related compliance costs relative to domestic industries, while also arguing
that COP is not the appropriate forum to discuss trade matters. Despite this, a European
Parliament briefing noted trade is a point of “exposure” for the EU at COP31.

COP30’s agreement on trade dialogue was notable, but it may not be enough
COP30 agreed three annual climate-trade dialogues at the mid-year sessions in 2026-28, with
a high-level event in 2028. The first, in Bonn (13 June), was calmer than expected but exposed
the divide: developing countries argued that climate-linked trade measures such as CBAM
raise costs and restrict market access and called for impact assessments within the UNFCCC,
while the EU defended them as responses to environmental externalities. With trade also

raised in other Bonn rooms, it is far from clear the dialogue will contain the issue in Antalya.
The African Group of negotiators has requested a Trade dialogue on the formal agenda.
China has already said it intends to use COP31 to oppose cleantech trade barriers, with its vice
minister of the Ministry of Ecology and Environment saying the event should discuss “in depth”
the “new problem of unilateralism and protectionism undermining global collective efforts” on

climate. The African Group is also pushing for trade to be on the formal agenda. Ultimately,
trade talks could prove more consequential at COP31 than in previous years given inflationary
pressures and concerns over business competitiveness, but it is hard to assess the potential
impact other than possibly causing delays in agreement. However, even if the impact is
limited, the issue could shape negotiations elsewhere outside of the COP process.
Sources: European Parliament, Carbon Brief, Reuters

### Page 13

TOP I C 7 | G EO P O L I TI CS  & MU L TI L ATER AL I S M (1  o f 2 )
Multilateralism is at a crossroads
Geopolitical fragmentation and the US exit from the Paris Agreement make consensus among nearly 200 nations harder, encouraging “coalitions of the willing” (outside of the formal COP
negotiations) to pursue more ambitious climate goals. COP31’s success or failure in securing multilateral agreement or credible voluntary pledges could shape future COPs by showing which

approaches deliver progress. The US retreat from the Paris Agreement will also continue to have far-reaching implications for climate finance. Overall, the EU and China will be expected to lead.
The consensus agreement principle under strain
The United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) provides the
multilateral framework for COP, the forum where countries negotiate collective climate
decisions. The process is anchored by the view that all countries must act on climate change,

but the extent of their efforts depends on national circumstances. This puts the onus on
developed countries to lead. The question is whether this principle still holds against a more
fragmented backdrop and transactional diplomatic context as national budgets prioritize
security and business competitiveness. With the COP process still separating developed and
developing countries by their 1992 UNFCCC classifications, developed countries are continuing

to push for wealthier developing nations such as China and Saudi Arabia to play a greater role
on finance. This has been a major point of contention at recent summits.
The US stepping back has far-reaching implications
The US skipped COP30 and left the Paris Agreement. It will formally exit the UNFCCC in
February 2027. This retreat has weighed on broader climate-finance decisions, both from a
monetary sense and through the loss of negotiating power. While cities, states and businesses

from the US continue to support COP efforts (e.g., groups like America Is All In), the federal
administration’s absence has put more pressure on the EU and China to lead.
What does an evolving process look like?
Because formal COP decisions require consensus (or lack of objection), progress often stalls or
moves slowly. COP30 saw Brazil move items where there was strong, but not unanimous,
support such as the fossil fuel roadmap, to separate streams outside the formal COP process.

Countries disappointed with the lack of stronger mitigation action also launched a separate
summit on transitioning away from fossil fuels, held this past April in Colombia. While COP is
the flagship global forum, these actions demonstrate a shift towards “coalitions of the willing”
for issues where global agreement is not possible.
Sources: EU Council, EU Parliament, Carbon Brief, UN, WRI

TO P I C  7 | G EO P O L I TI C S  &  MU L TI L ATER AL I S M (2  o f 2 )
Much will rest on the approach of the EU and China

With the US absent, the EU and China are the two actors with the greatest diplomatic and economic leverage with which to broker compromise agreements between the different factions at
COP31. This will require careful diplomacy, with their interests not fully aligned in some areas; discussions around trade and climate finance may be particularly challenging. At the same time,
both have reiterated their continued support of climate action and multilateral decision making; a successful COP outcome would back up these commitments.

### Page 15

TO P I C  8 | AI  &  C L I MATE
The Antalya AI Pledge puts data-centre power demand on the agenda
We expect AI’s climate potential and rising electricity demand to make it a prominent COP31 topic, prompting debate over COPs’ role in data-centre energy use. The Antalya AI Pledge invites
governments and companies to commit voluntarily to sustainable infrastructure and environmental transparency. Its call for zero- and low-emissions energy infrastructure leaves room for

nuclear power, prompting discussion of its role in meeting data-centre demand alongside renewables.
The Antalya AI Pledge seeks to build stakeholder support for sustainable AI buildout
Türkiye is encouraging stakeholders (inc. corporates) to commit to the pledge, asking
signatories to “declare our shared ambition to make AI part of the climate solution. This
means sustainably powered, efficiently designed, transparently measured and available to all”.

Signatories will commit to: 1) Power and build AI infrastructure and hardware sustainably, 2)
Design, manage or procure AI responsibly across its lifecycle, 3) Support assessment and
reporting of the environmental impacts of AI systems, 4) Strengthen financial support and
technical infrastructure for AI for climate action.
The AI for Clean Technologies Initiative seeks to highlight AI climate solutions
The initiative plans to develop AI-enabled pilot projects (e.g. smart energy systems) and then

link the projects with industry partners and investors, aiming to demonstrate AI’s potential to
contribute to sustainability goals. For grids, the IEA sees AI improving forecasting, congestion
management and outage response, helping operators get more from existing lines.
The dialogue here will set the stage for AI discussions at future COPs
While the pledge is voluntary, many of these themes are already beginning to appear in early-
stage regulatory demands, with transparency and disclosure receiving particular focus. There

will be questions over how effective the pledge can be given the US has disengaged from the
COP process, though California could send a strong signal if it commits to the pledge. We will
be particularly focused on how the tech industry responds. Despite the trend of greenhushing,
we expect many to send delegations to Antalya, with AI companies and investors viewing
responsible practices and community engagement as key to avoiding backlash and delays to

AI buildout. Note that responsible AI was also a hot topic at the Principles for Responsible
Investment conference this year in October.
Sources: COP31 Presidency, IEA

### Page 16

TO P I C  9 | N ATU R E &  C AR B O N  MAR K ETS
Nature and carbon markets have key roles to play in supporting climate action
Carbon credit discussions will centre on government policies to boost demand and corporate engagement, with much of the formal UN architecture now in place. If carbon markets can scale,
the benefits would be wide-ranging (e.g. supporting emissions targets and providing finance for developing countries). Credibility and greenwashing concerns remain key risks however; we

expect prices to diverge in favour of high-integrity credits. The Tropical Forest Forever Facility (TFFF) now has ~$7.8bn of sponsor capital against a $10bn end-2026 target.
Sources: Tropical Forest Forever Facility, Coalition to Grow Carbon Markets
16
Voluntary carbon market development
Türkiye has stated a focus on carbon markets at COP31, particularly on government actions to
boost demand, highlighting the potential for carbon markets to unlock more than $50bn in

additional annual finance for developing nations. The Coalition to Grow Carbon Markets
(made up of 13 governments) has announced it will unveil a “Policy Playbook” at COP31 to
support market development. While much of Article 6 (carbon credits under UN process) has
moved to delivery, countries will aim to reach agreement on the funding model for Article 6.2
at COP31. Further carbon market progress stands to have multiple benefits; it can support
developed countries in hitting climate goals while reducing immediate decarbonisation

requirements, while also providing finance and supporting ecosystems in developing ones.
Understanding the pushback
Carbon markets have seen pushback over credit integrity (including regulatory scrutiny) and
the risk that offsets delay domestic cuts. UN involvement is needed to enforce common
accounting and integrity rules, such as the Article 6 rulebook finalised at COP29 and due for
review in 2028, that stop the same reduction being claimed twice. Without that backstop,

fragmented bilateral and voluntary schemes risk eroding the trust needed to scale the market.
Update on the TFFF
The Tropical Forest Forever Facility (TFFF), launched at COP30 last year, aims for $10bn of
sponsor contributions by year-end. It pays tropical-forest countries per hectare conserved,
discounts payments for each hectare lost or degraded, and directs at least 20% to Indigenous
Peoples and local communities. Its Charter was adopted on 22 July 2026 and received a

tailwind following the re-election of Brazilian President Lula, who is a key supporter. Shortly
after Lula’s re-election, the Netherlands pledged up to $500mn to the fund, bringing the total
pledged to $7.8bn. Further financing commitments are key, with Norway’s $3bn contribution
conditional on the fund reaching at least $10bn by the end of 2026.
Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Preview  |  October 2026

TO P I C  1 0 | P AC I F I C , O C EANS  & S EA L EV EL  R I S E
Oceans will be a priority item, with Australia promising to champion Pacific interests
Australia’s ask for ceding the hosting was a Pacific pre-COP, and it has promised to continue championing Pacific interests at COP31. The Taku Pakasoa Declaration, signed at the pre-COP,

reflects Pacific priorities, calling for faster emissions cuts, more adaptation finance and financial system reform. The climate-ocean nexus will also be a focus, including investable solutions.
Championing Pacific interests is a key focus for Australia
As part of the hosting compromise, Australia co-hosted the official pre-COP with Fiji and
Tuvalu (5–8 Oct), attended by countries including Germany, the UAE and India (though some
questioned the low number of international leaders who attended). Australia has pledged to

keep championing Pacific interests as President of the Negotiations.
Taku Pakasoa Declaration reflects Pacific Priorities
Signed on 6 Oct at the pre-COP, it calls for faster emissions cuts, a substantial increase in
climate finance (particularly for adaptation in capacity-constrained countries), reform of the
international financial architecture, and for elevating the ocean-climate nexus. It also
welcomes the 35% electrification goal.
The challenges of rising sea levels are gaining increasing prominence

Sea levels in the region are rising faster than the global average, and half of Pacific Island
infrastructure lies within 500 metres of the coast. The UN General Assembly adopted the UN
Declaration on Sea Level Rise by consensus on 24 Sept; global sea levels rose nearly 6mm in
2024, the highest annual increase on record.
Oceans are key to climate stability, but more finance is needed
Pacific nations emphasise the critical role of oceans as a natural environmental regulator.

They absorb 30% of all carbon dioxide emissions (largest carbon sink) and capture 90% of the
excess heat generated by these emissions. Investor interest in sustainable ocean solutions is
growing, with 2026 YTD blue bond issuance already an annual record, yet bond issuance
supporting UN SDG 14: Life Below Water trails many other categories; scaling this up will be a
focus at COP31. A commitment to delivering “tangible, bankable ocean outcomes” at COP31 is

one of 13 points included as part of the Trabzon Ocean Call to Action unveiled by Türkiye,
while the ORRAA has unveiled a Blue Climate Finance Pathway.
Sources: Taku Pakasoa Declaration (UNFCCC), UN Declaration on Sea Level Rise

priorities. COP33 falls to Asia-Pacific in 2028, with China reportedly considering a bid and South Korea potentially interested. COP31–33 will test delivery in a fragmented world, with a second

Appendix A: Key COP terms used in this report
This appendix explains the main COP process terms and negotiating concepts used throughout the report. Definitions are summarised for non-specialist readers and reflect how each term is
used in this deck.
Sources: UNFCCC; COP30 and COP31 Presidency documents; and the other sources referenced in this report
19
Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Preview  |  October 2026
TERM DEFINITION

Action Agenda Voluntary, non-binding pledges and initiatives launched alongside the formal talks. It is separate from negotiated decisions.
Annex II / Non-Annex I UNFCCC country classification. Annex II covers developed economies; Non-Annex I covers most other countries, including China and India.
Article 6 Paris Agreement framework for international carbon markets. Article 6.2 covers bilateral trading of carbon credits between countries.
Article 9

Paris Agreement finance provisions. Article 9.1 covers developed
countries’ obligation to provide finance. COP30 set a two-year work
programme for 2026-27.
Baku to Belém Roadmap
to $1.3T
Roadmap to scale climate finance to $1.3tn a year by 2035 from all
sources, inclusive of the $300bn NCQG.
Bonn sessions Mid-year technical talks of the UNFCCC Subsidiary Bodies (e.g. SB64) in Bonn, Germany, which prepare the ground for COP discussions.
Brazil fossil fuel roadmap COP30 Presidency’s voluntary roadmap for transitioning away from fossil fuels, pursued outside the formal UN negotiations.

Consensus How COP decisions are taken: adopted when no party formally objects, so a single party can theoretically block or dilute an outcome.
COP / COPs Conference of the Parties: the UNFCCC’s annual decision-making summit, where nearly 200 parties negotiate climate action.
COP Presidency The host country’s team that chairs the conference. For COP31, Türkiye’s Murat Kurum is COP President and leads the Action Agenda.
TERM DEFINITION
Final COP text Decisions adopted by consensus at the close of the COP, setting its formal outcome.

Formal agenda Negotiated items on the official COP agenda, decided by consensus. Separate from the voluntary Action Agenda.
Global Goal on
Adaptation (GGA)
Paris Agreement goal to strengthen resilience. COP30 adopted 59
indicators; the link to finance is contested.
Global Stocktake (GST) Five-yearly review of progress under the Paris Agreement. The second runs from COP31 to COP33 (2028).
Just transition
mechanism
Belém Action Mechanism agreed at COP30, focused on fairness in the

shift to a low-carbon economy.
Loss and damage fund Fund set up at COP27 and operationalised in 2023 to support vulnerable developing countries.
NCQG New Collective Quantified Goal (COP29): at least $300bn a year for developing countries by 2035, within a $1.3tn ambition.
NDC Nationally determined contribution: each country’s national climate plan, with emissions targets and actions, updated every five years.
Negotiating groups Blocs that coordinate national positions, such as the G77 & China, the EU, LDCs and AOSIS.

Paris Agreement 2015 treaty to hold warming well below 2°C and pursue 1.5°C, with national climate plans (NDCs) as its core mechanism.
Pre-COP Ministerial meeting ahead of the COP to test positions and build consensus. For COP31, Australia co-hosted a Pacific pre-COP.
President of Negotiations COP31 role held by Australia’s Chris Bowen, who drafts final texts and steers government-to-government talks.
UNFCCC UN Framework Convention on Climate Change: the 1992 treaty behind the climate process. Its parties meet annually at the COP.

Appendix C: The level of alarm over global warming has begun to move higher (again)
As part of our monthly Climate & Sustainability Survey, we ask consumers in the UK, US, Germany, France, Spain and Italy for their views on global warming. In Q3 2026, we recorded a notable
rebound in alarm regarding the pace of global warming, particularly in the UK and Europe following a summer of climate extremes. The level of alarm had begun to dip globally post the 2024 US

presidential election in November.

### Page 1

After resilient growth this year, several headwinds will likely weigh on the n
economy in 2027. We expect high energy prices to slow consumption over the 
winter, tighter credit conditions to curb demand and France to stagnate until the 
elections. But global manufacturing (supported by AI capex), German fiscal 
expansion and continued structural expansion in Southern Europe should 
cushion these headwinds. We expect Euro area growth of 0.9% in 2027, below 
the recent run rate, estimated potential and consensus.  
High energy prices will likely keep headline inflation near 4% through the winter, n
while indirect effects push core inflation to a 2.9% peak in Q2. But we expect a 
short-lived hump given limited wage second-round effects, with sequential 
inflation pressures largely normalising in 2027H2. 
Above-target inflation and recent ECB commentary point to one more rate hike n
in December. A March move is possible with higher energy prices and stronger 
data, but the hurdle to faster hikes is high. We expect normalisation cuts from 
2027Q4 as the inflation shock fades, leaving our rate path below market pricing. 
Intensifying French budget tensions are the main risk. Polls suggest that Marine n
Le Pen is most likely to become President. If she is elected and wins a majority in 
Parliament, investors could question her commitment to reduce the deficit. If the 
elections fail to deliver a parliamentary majority, the prospects of continued high 
deficits could further weigh on domestic assets. French fiscal tensions could spill 
over into other high-debt countries, but the risk of area-wide contagion looks 
notably lower than in 2011-15. 
Europe also faces structural headwinds from lost competitiveness, rising n
competition from China and demographic changes. Growth industries (such as 
defence), widespread AI adoption and further internal market integration) offer 
significant opportunities, although the upcoming elections will likely hinder 
major reform progress in the near term.

### Page 2

Euro Area 2027 Outlook: Resilience Put to the Test

Following surprisingly resilient Euro area growth this year, we expect 2027 to be more 
challenging. We forecast area-wide growth of 0.9% next year, slightly below the recent 
run rate, our estimate of potential and consensus (Exhibit 1). But we expect growth to 
improve in H2 as headwinds fade and see significant variation across the Euro area, with 
firm growth in Germany and Spain, subdued momentum in Italy and stagnation in 
France.

Rising Headwinds 
We expect several headwinds to slow growth next year, especially in H1. 
First, high energy prices are likely to weigh on consumption over the winter (Exhibit 2). 
Our commodity strategists expect Brent oil and European TTF gas prices to remain 
elevated given continued disruption of Middle East supplies, with risks towards renewed 
escalation and higher prices. As a result, high headline inflation is likely to weigh 
materially on real disposable income over the winter and slow consumer spending. 
Consistent with this, consumer confidence has dropped since the summer and retail 
sales have weakened, especially in Germany.

Exhibit 1: : We Expect Trend-Like Growth in 2027

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics, Bloomberg
13 November 2026   2
Goldman Sachs European Economics Analyst

### Page 3

Second, tighter credit conditions look set to slow growth (Exhibit 3). Following recent 
easing, our area-wide financial condition index (GSFCI) is now only modestly tighter 
since the summer, as a weaker trade-weighted Euro has mitigated the effects of higher 
long-term yields and wider credit spreads. But survey-based measures of bank lending 
have deteriorated notably, especially in France. As a result, credit conditions are likely to 
act as a moderate headwind to growth next year, particularly on rate-sensitive domestic 
activity (such as housing and business investment).

Third, we expect the French economy to stagnate until the elections (Exhibit 4). In 
addition to tightening financial conditions, heightened political and policy uncertainty 
are likely to lead households and firms to postpone major consumption and investment 
decisions. Fiscal policy is likely to remain a contractionary force, because a large 
increase in interest payments will likely mask a modest consolidation in the primary 
balance. We forecast annual average growth of 0.1% in 2027, well below other

Exhibit 2: Higher Energy Prices are Likely to Weigh on Consumption

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics, Bloomberg

Exhibit 3: Tighter Credit Conditions Will Weigh on Growth

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics
13 November 2026   3
Goldman Sachs European Economics Analyst

### Page 4

forecasters and the weakest forecast among the developed economies we cover.

Sources of Resilience 
That said, we see several sources of resilience, which should buffer these headwinds and 
help growth recover in H2. 
First, we expect a continued boost from the global manufacturing cycle (Exhibit 5). The 
international industrial upswing continues, boosting factory orders and exports, 
especially in Germany. AI capex is an important driver behind this pickup, likely lifting 
Euro area growth by 0.1pp this year and next. Given spare capacity in European 
manufacturing—particularly in Germany—we see a continued cyclical export tailwind 
ahead.

Second, fiscal policy will continue to support growth in Germany (Exhibit 6). We remain 
constructive on the German fiscal rollout driven by higher defence spending and

Exhibit 4: We Expect Stagnation in France as Fiscal Uncertainty Builds

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics

Exhibit 5: A Continued Boost from Global Manufacturing, Including AI Capex

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics
13 November 2026   4
Goldman Sachs European Economics Analyst

### Page 5

improved execution of infrastructure investment. We expect the German deficit to rise 
to 4.6% next year (from 4% in 2026) and boost German growth by 0.6pp (vs 0.7pp this 
year). That said, fiscal policy looks set to turn contractionary in France and Spain as 
national consolidation pressures build and the Recovery Fund expires. As a result, we see 
a neutral area-wide fiscal stance in 2027 after a small net boost this year, before turning 
contractionary in 2028.

Third, we see continued structural expansion in Southern Europe (Exhibit 7). Spain, in 
particular, has continued to outgrow the Euro area materially, driven by strong gains in 
employment (underpinned by immigration), higher-value-added services (including 
finance and ICT) and investment. While momentum is likely to moderate next year, we 
see continued outperformance in the South.

Exhibit 6: Fiscal Policy Will Remain Expansionary in Germany, But Slow Growth Elsewhere

Exhibit 7: Continued Structural Expansion in Southern Europe

### Page 6

Modest Underlying Inflation Pressures 
High energy prices (including product spreads) are likely to keep headline inflation close 
to 4% through the winter (Exhibit 8). Headline inflation could climb even higher if upside 
risks to our commodity price forecasts materialise, with headline inflation peaking close 
to 6% in an adverse scenario for oil and gas. We also see building pressures on food 
inflation next year, reflecting passthrough effects from higher energy prices, the ongoing 
Russia/Ukraine war and global weather patterns.

We do not, however, look for material passthrough effects from headline to underlying 
inflation (Exhibit 9). So far, we see little evidence of indirect effects of higher energy 
prices into core inflation, with muted movement in energy-intensive core goods prices. 
Instead, we estimate that most of the recent pickup in core goods inflation reflects 
AI-related equipment. Given pipeline pressures in import and producer prices as well as 
airfares, we expect more notable indirect effects to build in coming months, pushing 
core inflation up.

Exhibit 8: A Sharp Pickup in Headline Inflation

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics
13 November 2026   6
Goldman Sachs European Economics Analyst

### Page 7

But we expect the core inflation hump to be short-lived given signs of limited 
second-round effects (Exhibit 10). Measures of wage growth have remained contained 
despite the surge in energy prices, including forward-looking surveys (such as the Indeed 
and ECB wage trackers) and recent wage agreements. While we have slightly raised our 
forecast for nominal wage growth next year, we expect pay gains to return to their 
normal range in 2028, driven by moderating labour demand.

Taken together, we expect core inflation to peak at 2.9% in Q2, significantly above the 
ECB’s target and somewhat above the recent staff projections (Exhibit 11), partly 
reflecting firmer energy price assumptions. But we expect the overshoot to be notably 
more limited and temporary than in 2022-23, with sequential inflation pressures largely 
normalising in 2027H2.

Exhibit 9: Modest Passthrough Effects Ahead

Source: Bloomberg, Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research

Exhibit 10: Little Risk of Second-Round Effects

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics, ECB
13 November 2026   7
Goldman Sachs European Economics Analyst

### Page 8

Limited ECB Tightening 
Above-target inflation, ongoing growth and signs that the neutral rate might have edged 
up point to additional ECB tightening, consistent with recent ECB communication and 
our macro model simulations (Exhibit 12). We therefore view one more hike at the 
December meeting as likely. Acute sovereign stress could still lead the Council to hold 
rates in December, but the required tightening in financial conditions would likely need 
to be large given the ongoing energy pressures.

An additional hike in March is possible if energy prices continue to rise, core inflation 
firms more than expected, or clearer signs of second-round effects emerge. But we see a 
significant hurdle to speeding up the pace of hikes and raising the deposit rate 
meaningfully above 3%. We expect the Governing Council to start normalising the policy 
rate as the inflation shock fades and sequential core inflation normalises, with cuts in

Exhibit 11: A Limited Inflation Overshoot

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics

Exhibit 12: A December Hike is Very Likely

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics
13 November 2026   8
Goldman Sachs European Economics Analyst

### Page 9

December 2027 and March 2028. As a result, our policy rate path remains below market 
pricing, including on a probability-weighted basis.

Risks from a French Budget Crisis 
An intensification of French budget tensions is the main downside risk to our outlook. 
French bonds could already come under stress before the elections if presidential 
candidates do not express sufficient concern regarding the fiscal outlook. Markets would 
probably react negatively to far-left leader Mélenchon progressing to the second round 
of the presidential elections, even though polls suggest he would then be very likely to 
lose against far-right leader Marine Le Pen. Investors could also question Le Pen’s 
commitment to reducing the deficit if she becomes President—as polls currently suggest 
she will—and wins a parliamentary majority. In that respect, we noted that her recent 
economic proposals would likely face significant implementation challenges, as they are 
based on optimistic growth assumptions and include costly measures. Finally, if no 
parliamentary majority emerges after the next elections—to which our model attributes 
a 20% probability— bond markets would likely react negatively to the prospect of 
continued high deficits, political uncertainty and low growth. As a result, we expect 
French government bonds to remain under pressure.

Exhibit 13: Our ECB Views Remain Dovish Compared with Market Pricing

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, ECB
13 November 2026   9
Goldman Sachs European Economics Analyst

### Page 10

An escalation of French budget tensions could spill over into other high-debt bond 
markets (Exhibit 15). Fiscal imbalances are elevated, interest rates are high, and debt 
dynamics look concerning in several countries (including Italy, Belgium, Austria and 
Finland). Financial-market vulnerabilities also persist through sovereign exposures and 
non-bank leverage. Upcoming elections in Italy may complicate the fiscal adjustment 
required, while Spain’s fiscal outlook remains encouraging (despite the November 29 
election). Also, failure to agree on the 2028-35 EU Budget could bring into question 
fiscal cohesion.

That said, the Euro area today is stronger in several important respects than during the 
European sovereign debt crisis (Exhibit 16). Nominal growth is materially higher, 
household finances are more robust, external imbalances are smaller, and banks—
especially in Southern Europe—are much healthier. The Euro area has also strengthened 
its institutional architecture through the ESM and greater common EU issuance.

Exhibit 14: : The Risk of a French Budget Crisis

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics, Polymarket

Exhibit 15: European Sovereign Risk is Back in Focus

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics, IMF
13 November 2026   10
Goldman Sachs European Economics Analyst

### Page 11

Moreover, the ECB could activate its anti-fragmentation tools in the event of extreme 
bond market pressures (Exhibit 17), including the Transmission Protection Instrument 
(TPI). But our analysis suggests that ECB intervention would likely require significantly 
more intense and broad-based sovereign stress, especially with inflation well above 
target. In particular, our new index—constructed to predict ECB bond market 
interventions—suggests that fragmentation risk remains limited despite the recent 
widening in spreads.

Structural Challenges and Opportunities  
Europe continues to struggle with several structural headwinds. The Euro area has lost 
international competitiveness and faces rising competition with China, which is likely to 
act as a steady headwind and push the EU towards a more assertive trade stance. Also, 
Europe has underinvested in growth sectors, offers a less attractive business

Exhibit 16: The Euro Area is Stronger than in 2011-15

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics

Exhibit 17: Fragmentation Risk Remains Limited

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics, Polymarket
13 November 2026   11
Goldman Sachs European Economics Analyst

### Page 12

environment than its DM peers and lacks cross-country harmonisation in regulation. 
Given a significant demographic headwind, we see area-wide potential growth at only 
1%, and declining later in the decade.

That said, we also see significant growth opportunities through reforms, as outlined in 
the Draghi report. In particular, Europe could improve its economic performance 
through well-targeted public investment (including defence), leadership in growth 
industries (such as pharmaceuticals and green technologies) and widespread adoption 
of AI. Estimates also point to significant growth upside from financial market reform 
(building on Germany’s pension reform) and further integration of the internal market 
(including EU Inc to support firm creation).

While the upcoming national elections will likely complicate major progress in coming 
months, it remains essential that policymakers push reforms that lead to a lasting

Document 383: OpenAI预训练指标曝光：27年AI将雪崩式爆发

### Page 1

OpenAI ⾸席科学家 Jakub Pachocki 发表重磅⻓⽂，直⾔： OpenAI 内部模型已越过数学突
破线，全⾏业应该为  AI 模型设置安全线！他还警告： 2027 年， AI 将迎来「雪崩式爆发」，
内部模型智⼒指数已逼近⼈类盲区！
摘要：
就在刚刚， OpenAI 罕⻅把⾃家核⼼机密摊开！
⾸席科学家  Jakub Pachocki 发表重磅⻓⽂，直⾔：
OpenAI 内部模型已越过数学突破线，全⾏业应该为  AI 模型设置安全线！
他警告： 2027 年， AI 将迎来「雪崩式爆发」！内部模型智⼒指数已逼近⼈类盲区！
仔细看下⾯这张「 AI 智商曲线」。
Log-Perplexity 越低，模型就越聪明。
当这个数值跌破 0.76 ， AI 解出千禧年数学难题，甚⾄开始⾃⼰参与新模型设计。
当它跌到 0.68-0.70 ， AI 的智商就进⼊绝对盲区。会出现什么颠覆性能⼒，没⼈知道。
⽽眼下，图表显⽰， OpenAI 内部模型已经达到了 0.73 ，距离⼈类盲区只有⼀步之遥。

OpenAI 提议：全⾏业都该刹⻋了！ AGI 的雪崩就在眼前！
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⻅闻历 搜索

### Page 2

奥特曼：这是 OpenAI 最重要的发布
OpenAI 核⼼⾼管也集体下场。
奥特曼直接定调：
这是  OpenAI ⼀段时间以来发布的最重要的内容。 ”
他说， OpenAI 决定公开预训练进展的⼤量数据，希望全⾏业能就「共同限制」达成⼀致，确保
安全与可控。
紧随其后，联合创始⼈  Greg Brockman 警告： AI 虽然也会帮⼈类做对⻬和监测，但安全进展不
太可能追得上预训练的加速。
在此之前，没有任何⼀家前沿实验室愿意公开⾃家的预训练损失（ loss ）曲线。那是模型能⼒的
底牌，⼀旦亮出来，竞争对⼿⼀眼就能看穿你离下⼀代模型还有多远。
Pachocki 坦⾔，公开这些数字确实会让对⼿摸清  OpenAI 的家底。
但他认为，眼下建⽴全⾏业共同安全底线的紧迫性，已经压过了商业保密。
再往下⾛⼀格，就是⼀⽚标着问号的灰⾊地带。 OpenAI 想在这⾥，踩下全⾏业的刹⻋线，
读懂 AI 的「智⼒刻度尺」：什么是困惑度？

### Page 3

要看懂  OpenAI 的恐惧，我们⾸先得认识⼀个词：困惑度（ Perplexity ）。
Jakub 指出，现代  AI 的底层逻辑其实简单粗暴得可怕：它就是⼀个在海量数据上训练出来的
「超级压缩机」（或预测器）。
困惑度，衡量就是这个模型预测下⼀个词的能⼒。数字越低，说明模型在⾯对数据时越「不困
惑」，预测越精准，它的智⼒就越⾼。
OpenAI 第⼀次公开从  GPT-3.5 到内部绝密模型的困惑度。
在这把尺⼦上，每⼀个数字的跌破，都代表着  AI 能⼒的⼀次恐怖⻜跃：
GPT-3.5 跌破  0.99——AI 突然具备了流利的对话能⼒，引爆了整个  ChatGPT 狂潮。
GPT-4 跌破  0.91 。 AI 突然学会了「思维链（ CoT ）推理」，开始像⼈⼀样⼀步步思考问题。
⽽  AI 跌破  0.80 ，则开始颠覆⽹络安全和数学领域。
请注意，以上都是过去式。接下来的数字，才是让  OpenAI 真正感到战栗的开始。
在过去的很⻓⼀段时间⾥，数学⼀直是  AI 的弱项。但  Jakub 透露了⼀个令⼈震惊的内部细节：

过去⼀年多， OpenAI ⼀直在⽤未解的开放性数学难题来测试模型。
当内部模型的对数困惑度压缩到  ~0.76 时，奇迹发⽣了。
对于我们这些曾在数学及相关领域⼯作过的⼈来说，看着模型在⼀个  Slack 群聊⾥，把我们思
考了多年的数学难题⼀个接⼀个地给出解答，那种感觉极其超现实。
模型开始产⽣突破性的全新结果！
这带来了⼀个可怕的推论：如果  AI 能在数学上做出突破，那么它完全有能⼒⾃⼰研究如何训练
下⼀代  AI ！
⼀旦  AI 介⼊预训练算法的研发，⼈类传统的研发节奏将被彻底打碎。

### Page 4

Jakub 预测：最快在  2027 年，我们将看到基础预训练进展的「雪崩式爆发」！
逼近 0.73 ，遥望 0.68 ：⼈类的「绝对盲区」
图表显⽰， OpenAI 的内部模型⽬前已经悄悄⼲到了  0.73 左右。
⽽下⼀个临界点，是  0.68 到  0.70 这个区间。
在这个区间会发⽣什么？ OpenAI 的⾸席科学家坦⽩地承认：「我不知道。」
当指标跌⼊这个窗⼝时，将会涌现出⼈类⽬前连「测试⽅法都还没发明出来」的颠覆性能⼒。
现有的安全对⻬⼿段、监控⽅法，在这个级别的智能⾯前，可能完全失效。
这就像掉进了⿊洞，你只知道⽇益逼近奇点，却不知道奇点之后是景象。
正 因 如 此 ， Jakub 和  Altman 提 出 了 ⼀ 个 震 动 业 界 的 疯 狂 提 议 ： 全 ⾏ 业 「 智 商 刹 ⻋ 线 」
（ Perplexity Limits ）。
他们呼吁所有前沿模型开发者联合承诺：把「困惑度」作为全⾏业的通⽤指标。

### Page 5

⼤家共同设定⼀个「红线」（⽐如  0.68 ），任何实验室的内部模型在触及这条红线时，必须强制
停⽌向更⾼智能的突破！
除⾮⼈类开发出了新⼀代的「监控器」，证明我们完全有能⼒理解和控制这种级别的  AI ⾏为，
否则，谁也不许继续踩油⻔。
⻢斯克群嘲：预训练就是训练，何必掩⽿盗铃？
OpenAI 的这番提议，瞬间在  X 上引爆了舆论。
⻢斯克也⽕速赶到评论区。
对于  OpenAI 把重点放在「预训练」上的说法，⻢斯克直接嘲讽：
预训练应该直接叫『训练』。你不能只有『预先』和『事后』，却从来没有真正的训练。
这就好⽐你换上全套运动服准备健⾝，然后直接去洗了个澡，但压根就没锻炼。
不过，嘲讽归嘲讽，⻢斯克对  AI 失控的恐惧丝毫不亚于  OpenAI 。
他指出了明年  AI 发展的真正瓶颈 ——数据枯竭。
当数据被榨⼲，算⼒却在呈指数级暴涨时，会发⽣什么？
⻢斯克留下⼀句让⼈细思极恐的预⾔：
" 想象⼀下，当我们达到百亿亿次级算⼒时，超级智能会是什么样⼦。到那时，（现在的  AI ）就

像是⼭顶洞⼈在玩树枝。 "
这把「刻度尺」完美吗？
当然，⽤「困惑度」来当红线，并不是完美⽆缺的。
Jakub 在⻓⽂中，也坦诚地列出了将困惑度作为「⾏业安全指标」的四个巨⼤挑战。

### Page 6

471 收藏 分享到 :
1. 跨实验室⽐较难：  ⼤家⽤的测试数据集和训练数据配⽅都不⼀样。哪怕指标⼀样，你的  0.7
和我的  0.7 可能代表不同的智⼒⽔平。
2. 脱离具体任务：  困惑度只是⼀个底层的预测指标，它不能直接反映  AI 写代码或者写⽂章的具
体能⼒。我们之所以关⼼它，是因为只有它在降低，才会「涌现」出全新的不可预测的能⼒。
3. ⽆法衡量预训练之外的进步：  ⽐如⼤放异彩的「思维链推理」，它让模型变聪明了⽆数倍，但
它并不会降低模型本⾝的困惑度数值。
4. 探不到底的深渊：  损失不可能降到  0 ，那意味着完全确定地记住所有数据，但我们不知道数
据的固有熵到底是多少。越接近极限，困惑度的下降会越来越慢，但哪怕只下降  0.01 ， AI 智能
的跃升都将是⼏何级的爆炸！

尽管有这些瑕疵，但  OpenAI 依然坚信，这是⽬前全⼈类能找到的唯⼀⼀把经受住时间考验、
能持续衡量通⽤智能增⻓的「尺⼦」。
红线的定义权，⼜回到巨头⼿⾥？
Altman 转发这篇⻓⽂，背后是⼀场关于「未来  AI 控制权」的博弈，赶在 Anthropic 上市这⼏
天，时机就更巧妙了。
可是，刹⻋线是实验室⾃⼰画出来的，这意味着「何时该停⽌」的定义权，还是在头部 AI 巨头
⼿⾥。
2027 年的雪崩已经进⼊倒计时。
⼈类第⼀次把通往未知智能的路程，折算成了⼀串清晰递减的数字：
0.99 、 0.91 、 0.87 、 0.83 、 0.76 、 0.73……
倒计时已经摆在所有⼈的眼前。在  2027 年的「雪崩」落下之前，这群⼿⾥攥着千亿美⾦的科技
巨头，真能⼀起松油⻔吗？
OpenAI 已经声称⾃⼰踩下了⼀半刹⻋。
接下来，就看有没有⼈愿意⼀起踩了。

SOVEREIGN DEBT: WHEN SHORT-TERM SHOCKS AND SHIFTS IN DEBT STRUCTURE LEAVE A LASTING FISCAL
BURDEN — THE CASE OF BRAZIL
Salim Hammad
Brazil’s recent post-election yield shock risks leaving a lasting imprint on public finances — compounded by already high interest rates and a
changing debt structure. Large refinancing needs and a rising share of floating-rate debt will transmit rapidly and significantly higher interest

rates to the debt service through distinct but reinforcing channels.
SOURCES : MACROBOND, BCB

CHART 1
An abrupt post-election market awakening

In the wake of the 2 nd round of the general elections in late October, Brazilian markets — which had expected a Flavio Bolsonaro
victory — sold off sharply after Luiz Inacio Lula da Silva won a fourth presidential term. Both the equity and FX markets
experienced large intraday drops (14% of the Ibovespa stock index and -11% BRL vs. USD). Meanwhile, the yield on Brazil’s 10-year
government bond rose 250 basis points to 15.5%, reaching its highest level in over a decade (Chart 1) as investors worried about

the fiscal impact of costly campaign promises amidst large and persistent fiscal imbalances.
Fiscal risks have reshaped Brazil’s public debt structure, magnifying the cost of persistently high interest rates
Absent a confidence-infusing fiscal adjustment, Brazil’s interest bill is unlikely to decline in the short term, maintaining upward
pressure on public debt (83% of GDP in October 2026) and limiting budgetary leeway.
Three transmission channels, amplified by changing features of the debt structure, explain this persistence.

The first channel: The rise in long-term yields will increase rollover costs.
The sovereign must refinance roughly 20% of its debt stock each year. An average maturity of 4.1 years spreads refinancing over
time but does not shield the interest bill from a sustained rise in borrowing costs. As maturing securities are replaced, higher
issuance rates progressively lift the average borrowing cost — even for fixed-rate debt, whose interest costs remain unchanged

until refinancing. A sustained yield shock therefore feeds into debt servicing as the stock rolls over.

### Page 2

2
The bank for
a changing
world
Chart of the Week / 10 November 2026 economic-research.bnpparibas.com
The second channel: Demand for floating-rate instruments will strengthen until confidence in fiscal policy improves.
In turn, a larger share of SELIC-linked debt heightens the debt stock’s sensitivity to the prevailing high policy rate. Already SELIC-linked
securities reached a record 52.6% of the debt stock in August 2026, around 12 percentage points above their share at the start of President

Lula’s last term, versus a low of 18.4% in September 2014 (Chart 2). Weaker demand for fixed-rate securities with maturities exceeding five
years, amid increased fiscal uncertainty, has encouraged this shift 1.
The third channel: The BCB may delay rate cuts until fiscal policy over the next mandate is better defined.
A slower easing cycle would prolong high interest costs on SELIC-linked debt, whose servicing costs adjust with the policy rate without

waiting for refinancing. With these securities accounting for a growing share of the debt stock, delayed monetary easing would postpone
relief for the Treasury’s interest bill — even if long-term market yields subsequently ease.
These channels operate at different speeds: a rise in long-term yields increases rollover costs, whereas floating-rate debt reprices with
the SELIC rate, not automatically with 10-year yields. Meanwhile, a slower monetary easing cycle and stronger demand for floating-rate

securities compound pressure on the interest bill.
The fiscal–monetary policy nexus is therefore likely to remain a source of strain on Brazil’s public finances: fiscal uncertainty could delay
monetary easing, while greater floating-rate exposure would prolong the burden of high policy rates on the interest bill.
salim.hammad@bnpparibas.com
1 Fiscal slippage and uncertainty make investors less willing to lock in nominal returns over long horizons: if inflation or market yields rise, the purchasing power of fixed payments

declines or the bonds’ market value falls. Investors therefore demand higher yields to bear this risk, making long-term fixed-rate funding more expensive for the Treasury. By issuing
floating-rate bonds instead, the Treasury offers returns that adjust with the SELIC rate, sustaining demand, securing financing and reducing the risk of failed auctions. However
longer floating-rate maturities may reduce rollover risk, but do not remove the repricing exposure. Floating-rate issuance helps secure financing but transfers interest-rate risk to

the Treasury.
SOURCES : NATIONAL TREASURY, BNP PARIBAS

Document 427: US SECTORS CALL_ Information Technology, Communication Services & Financials

Source PDF: all report pdf/US SECTORS CALL_ Information Technology, Communication Services & Financials.pdf
Report source: researchmail轉寄
Date: 2026-11-23

Status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: US SECTORS CALL: Information Technology, Communication Services & Financials
Date:    1 hour ago
US SECTORS CALL: Information Technology, Communication Services & Financials

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
US SECTORS CALL: Information
Technology, Communication
Services & Financials
By Ed Yardeni & Toby Hearst • 22 Nov 2026
View in browser
Photo by Steve A Johnson / Unsplash

### Page 2

Last week was brutal. All 11 S&P 500 sectors fell sharply. The S&P 500
dropped 3.7% on Friday alone. The index has fallen for seven days in a
row, its longest losing streak since February 2020. It is up just 1.0% ytd.
The Nasdaq is on its longest weekly losing streak since 2008. The S&P
500 Semiconductors index is down 28.1% mtd.
Last week, we compared November's Friday the 13th S&P 500 close to
August 3, when the index first rose to the same level. We noted that while

the index was relatively calm over that period, there was lots of turbulence
below the surface in the S&P 500 sectors. The calm broke last week. The
S&P 500 is down 9.1% since August 3, and only three sectors remain
above water. Health Care (OW) and Consumer Staples (UW) are up 9.2%
and 8.6% (chart). Energy (OW) is up 2.8% and remains the best sector
performer ytd at a 34.8% gain. Communication Services (MW) is down
19.7%, with Information Technology (MW) and Industrials (OW) down

15.2% and 14.7%.

### Page 3

So far, the selloff has been driven more by increasingly bearish valuation
multiples than by analysts' consensus earnings estimates, which remain
bullish. Let's have a closer look at Information Technology,
Communication Services, and Financials:
(1) Information Technology. Investors don't believe the analysts'
earnings forecasts. Analysts expect IT earnings to grow 58.4% this year
and 48.4% next year. They expect Semiconductors earnings to grow
114.6% and 77.8% this year and next and Semiconductor Equipment

earnings to grow 36.3% and 51.3%. Investors are not paying for that. The
forward P/E of the sector is down 41.9% ytd, while forward earnings is up
71.0% (chart). The stock price index is down 0.7%, after rising over 30%
in early October.

### Page 4

Semiconductors show the gap most clearly. The industry accounts for
54.9% of IT's forward earnings and 38.0% of its market cap. Its forward
P/E has fallen to 9.4, well below the S&P 500's 16.6, and is near its 2010-
11 lows (chart).
Semiconductor Equipment is no different. Its forward earnings is up 89.7%
ytd, while its forward P/E is down 41.8% (chart).
Application Software is the exception. Analysts cut its 2027 earnings
growth forecast to 9.5% from about 17% in August (chart). It is down

28.6% ytd, the worst in the Information Technology industry.

### Page 5

We lowered our sector recommendation to market-weight late last year.
We are sticking with it because we believe the AI buildout will survive CEO
Jensen Huang's resignation from Nvidia.
(2) Communication Services. Meta and Alphabet make up about 40% of
the sector. Both have been caught up in the AI selloff. Meta is down 28%
from its ytd peak, and Alphabet is down 34%. The sector is down 15.5%
ytd, the worst of all 11 S&P 500 sectors. Forward earnings is up 21.5%

ytd, while the forward P/E is down 26.0% (chart).
Analysts expect Interactive Media & Services revenues to grow 25.8% in
2027. Yet they expect earnings to fall 14.2% (chart). Much of that reflects
a base effect from this year's mark-to-market gains posted by Alphabet.

### Page 6

The forward profit margin has also dropped to 26.4% from almost 32%
this summer, partly reflecting the reversal of the mark-to-market impact
next year.
We retain our market-weight rating on Communication Services because a
slower pace of the AI buildout should boost the profitability and cash flow
of the Magnificent-7 including Alphabet and Meta.
(3) Financials. Investors are treating parts of the sector as an AI trade,
which has dragged the whole group down. Investment Banking &

Brokerage fell 11.7% this week, trading down in step with IT. Last week,
we noted how the sector's forward P/E had fallen to 15.2. It is now 14.2,
down from 16.4 at the end of 2025 (chart). Forward earnings is still up
14.1% ytd.

### Page 7

Insurance has been the shelter from the storm. The Property & Casualty
Insurance price index is near a record high, and Insurance Brokers trades
11.8% above its 200-day moving average. Property & Casualty
Insurance's forward profit margin of 13.7% is the highest since 2007
(chart). Analysts expect earnings to grow 11.9% this year but to decline
8.4% next year, so the margin is likely peaking.
Consumer Finance is especially at risk. The industry’s price index is down

21.6% ytd, with a forward P/E that has dropped to 10.2. Yet analysts
expect that industry’s earnings to grow 11.3% this year and 11.4% next
year (chart). The industry is getting cheaper even as earnings grow.
Investors worry that Washington will cap credit card interest rates next
year.

### Page 8

Analysts expect Financials' earnings growth to slow to 7.4% next year
from 16.9% this year. Some de-rating makes sense. But with the sector
trading at 14.2 times forward earnings, we think the market has already
priced in the slowdown. We retain our overweight rating on Financials.

JAPAN RESEARCH INSTITUTE
1
保険業界の規制改革とインド経済
インド政府は、過去四半世紀にわたり国民生活の安定と金融仲介機能の強化に向けて保険市場の自由化を
推進してきたが、ここにきて消費者保護強化に向けた規制強化にかじを切ろうとしている。
■規制緩
和を受けてインドの保険市場は急成長
インド政府は、国民生活の安定と金融仲介機
能の強化に向けて、保険市場の発展を推進して
きた。この背景としては、以下の 2 点を指摘で
きる。
第 1 に、インドでは社会保障制度が整備途上
にある。そのため、予期せぬ疾病や事故をきっ
かけとする世帯所得の急減リスクを抑制するう
えで、医療保険や生命保険への加入が極めて重
要なセーフティネットとなる。
第 2 に、持続的なインド経済の発展のために
は、預入期間の短い銀行預金とは別に、インフ
ラ整備や産業育成の原動力となる長期かつ安定
的な国内資本の蓄積が必要である。保険会社が

集めて運用する巨額の資金は、まさにこの長期
資金供給の担い手として期待されてきた。
これらの目的の達成に向け、インド政府は過
去四半世紀にわたって段階的な規制緩和を進め
てきた（右上表）。2000 年の民間企業や外資企
業の参入自由化を皮切りに、2002 年のバンカシ
ュアランス（銀行窓口での保険販売）解禁、外
資出資比率上限の引き上げなどを進めた。
こうした一連の規制緩和によって多数の民
間・外国企業が参入し、市場競争が活性化した。
日本からも日本生命や東京海上グループを含め、
複数の保険会社が参入し、対印投資を牽引した。
市場参入や販売に関わる規制緩和に加え、中
間所得層の拡大、家計における金融リテラシー
の向上、そして保険サービスのデジタル化も相
まって、インドの保険市場は飛躍的な拡大を遂
げた。保険料収入は経済成長率を上回るペースで拡大し、近年の保険会社の運用資産残高は GDP
の約 4 分の 1 に達している（右下図）。
インド政府は、独立 100 周年を迎える 2047 年までに全国民が自らのリスクに適した保険に手

ごろな価格でアクセスできる「Insurance for All by 2047」の実現を目標に掲げており、今後もイ
ンドの保険市場は基本的に底堅い成長をたどると見込まれる。
上席主任研究員 熊谷 章太郎
(kumagai.shotaro@jri.co.jp）

用）

JAPAN RESEARCH INSTITUTE
2
■当局が規制強化に舵を切る理由
一方で、急激な市場拡大の副作用として、構造
的な問題も顕在化している。最大の課題は保険販
売コストの高騰と不適切販売の横行である。保険
販売競争が激化するなか、保険会社が商業銀行や
ノンバンクといった販売代理店に対して支払う
手数料・報酬は、新規契約保険料を大幅に上回る
ペースで増加しており（右上図）、それが新規保
険料の上昇や将来の保険料支払いの減少につな
がることへの懸念が広がった。また、顧客のニー
ズに合致しない複雑な商品の強引な販売や、商品
に関する説明不足も社会問題化した。
こうした状況を重くみた IRDAI（イン
ド保険規制開発局）は、市場の健全性を
維持すべく、足元で消費者保護を主眼と
した規制強化を検討している。2026 年 9
月に公表した改革案では、①販売代理店
への報酬構造の見直し、②保険会社の事
業費・管理費に対する規制厳格化、③販

売手法に対する規制厳格化、④コンプラ
イアンスの徹底に向けた流通チャネルの
合理化、⑤監査や情報公開制度の厳格化、
などの方向性が示された（右下表）。改
革案に関する意見を広く募ったうえで、
2027 年前半に新たな制度を導入するとし
ている。
一連の規制強化の影響は、プラス面と
マイナス面に分けられる。まず、プラス
面としては、過度なインセンティブ競争
や不適切な販売が抑制されることで消費
者保護が大幅に強化され、保険市場の信
頼性と持続的発展の基盤が強化される点が挙げられる。
他方、マイナス面として懸念されるのは、移行期における副作用である。販売規制の厳格化に
伴い、保険会社および販売代理店である金融機関の収益拡大ペースの一時的な鈍化は避けられな
い。さらに、保険会社や金融機関がインドの金融市場における主たる投資主体であることを勘案
すると、規制強化が株式・債券価格に対して下押し圧力をもたらす公算が大きい。
保険市場のこうした変化は、今後のインド経済を展望するうえで重要な視点を提供する。すな

わち、保険業界に限らず多くの産業において、自由化による市場の急成長は様々な問題を生じさ
せ得るものの、そうした事態に対する規制の再設計・適正化こそが、新たな発展の礎になるとい
う点である。その嚆矢として保険をめぐる規制の行方が注目される。

生命保険

### Page 4

ASIA MONTHLY  2026 年 11 月（No.308） 
JAPAN RESEARCH INSTITUTE 
   3
専門人材の渡航制限が中国の成長を下押す恐れ 
中国政府は 9 月、新たな出入国管理規定を施行した。技術流出を防ぐための人材の囲い込みは、海外の知
識を取り込む機会を狭め、中長期的な経済成長を下押しする恐れがある。

■海外渡航制限の対象者を拡大 
中国政府は 9 月 15 日、出入国の条件や審査手
続きを具体化する新規定を施行した。新規定では、
輸出管理・技術輸出入の規則に違反し、国家の産
業・技術の安全を脅かす可能性がある中国国民に
対して、商務部などが出国禁止を決定できる旨が
明記された（右表）。 
中国では、2013 年に施行された出境入境管理
法に基づき、刑事事件の被告人・被疑者や、未解
決の民事事件について裁判所が出国を認めない
者などは、出国制限の対象となっていた。また、
中国国民については、国家の安全と利益を損なう
恐れがあることを理由に、関係当局が出国を認め
ない場合も規定されていた。出国禁止とは別に、
国家機密を扱う人員や公的部門の幹部らに対し、旅券の集中保管や渡航の事前承認による管理も
行われてきた。今回の新規定では、経済安全保障面での出国制限が明確化された形となる。 
こうしたなか、9 月末には、AI・半導体分野の重要人材に課されていた海外渡航制限が、配偶
者や子供などの家族にも拡大されたと報じられた。報道によれば、重要人材には、著名な AI スタ
ートアップ企業の創業者、大手民間企業の主要幹部、中核的な研究開発人材などが含まれる。一
律の出国禁止ではないものの、対象者とその家族は、短期の私的旅行や出張であっても、出国に
当局の許可が必要とされる。 
今回の規制強化の背景には、米中間の技術競争があると考えられる。生成 AI などの重要技術を
巡る競争が激しくなるなか、中国政府は、技術だけでなく、それを生み出す研究者や技術者も戦
略的な資産として重視しているとみられる。家族の渡航も管理することで、本人の海外移住や研
究開発拠点の移転を抑制する狙いがあると考えられる。今回の報道で注目されるのは、公的部門
に限らず、民間企業の重要人材やその家族にも管理が及んでいる点である。今回のような人材移
動の制約は、これまで中国の成長を支えてきた技術吸収と生産性向上のメカニズムを揺るがす恐
れがある。 
■市場開放と技術吸収に支えられた中国の生産性上昇 
経済成長に対して、生産性は大きな影響力を持つ。一国経済の生産効率を示す全要素生産性
（Total Factor Productivity, TFP ）は、資本や労働の投入量だけでは説明できない生産の効率性
を捉える推計指標であり、技術水準や資源配分の効率性などを反映する。 
中国の TFP は、長期的に上昇してきた（次頁右上図）。背景には、改革開放後の市場化や民間
企業の成長に加え、貿易・直接投資を通じて、海外の技術や経営ノウハウを取り込んできたこと
が指摘できる。加えて、農業から製造業などへの労働移動や、企業間の競争・新陳代謝も、経済
研究員 古宮 大夢 
(komiya.hiromu@jri.co.jp）

・ 関係当局が出国禁止を決定できる

　 かす可能性がある者

JAPAN RESEARCH INSTITUTE
4
全体の生産性向上を支えてきた。
もっとも、中国の TFP は主要国のなかでは依
然として低い水準にある（右下図）。Penn World
Table の推計によると、2023 年の中国の TFP は
米国の半分弱であり、ドイツをはじめとする欧
州主要国、韓国や日本はもとより、タイやマレ
ーシアの水準にも届いていない。背景には、非
効率な資源配分や先進的な技術・経営手法の普
及の遅れが挙げられる。国有企業への金融・制
度面での優遇、地方保護主義、企業の参入・退
出を妨げる仕組みなどが資源配分を歪め、資本
や労働が生産性の高い企業・部門で十分に活用
されていない。また、中国が電気自動車や半導
体などの一部の先端分野で高い競争力を持って
いても、そうした産業の先進的な技術・経営手
法が経済の大宗を担うサービス業や中小企業な
どには普及していないことも、全体の生産性向
上を制約する要因となっている。

■技術の囲い込みが中長期的に成長を阻害
技術流出を防ぐための人材の囲い込みは、生
産性の先進国へのキャッチアップを一層遅らせ
る恐れがある。専門人材の渡航制限には、国内
の研究開発人材を確保し、既存の技術的優位を
守る側面がある一方、海外の知識を吸収したり、
新たな技術を海外と共同で生み出したりする機会を狭める可能性がある。技術やノウハウの移転
は、論文や設計図といった「形式知」の共有だけで完結するものではなく、対面での議論や共同
作業、生産現場での経験を通じて共有される「暗黙知」が極めて重要である。渡航管理によって
国際学会への参加や共同研究、海外企業との技術交流が遅延・見送りとなれば、こうした知識を
吸収する機会が失われ、技術革新や生産工程の改善が遅れる恐れがある。さらに、渡航許可の取
得に時間がかかったり、許可の見通しが立ちにくくなったりすれば、技術者の海外派遣や国際的
な人材配置の障害にもなる。
今回施行された新規定は外国人の入国管理も対象としており、出入国管理の運用への不透明感

が高まれば、海外からの高度人材の招聘や定着にとっても障壁となる。すでに非効率な資源配分
や先進的な知見の普及の遅れが中国経済の生産性向上の重石となっているなか、海外との人的交
流まで制約されれば、知識の吸収・創出と、その活用がさらに滞る可能性がある。影響の大きさ
は対象範囲や実際の運用に左右されるものの、技術保護を過度に重視した人材の交流や渡航に関
する制限は中長期的に TFP の伸びを鈍らせ、中国経済の潜在成長率を下押しする逆風となり得る。

台 湾 AI ブームを背景に高成長が継続
■足元は内外需ともに好調
台湾景気は、内外需に支えられて拡大している。
外需の面では、世界的な AI 需要の高まりを受け
て米国向けの半導体や AI サーバーの輸出が急増
しており、輸出全体は前年同月比＋50％程度で推
移している。一方で、停滞が指摘されていた AI
ブームの恩恵を受けにくいその他製品の輸出も、
足元では 2 桁の伸びを見せている（右上図）。た
だし、その主因は輸出品の価格上昇である。実際、
それら製品を製造する産業の生産活動は横ばい
にとどまっており、本格的な回復とは言えない状
況である。
個人消費は、良好な雇用・所得環境に支えられ
て好調に推移している。製造業では、AI ブームに
押し上げられている電子部品産業のほか、幅広い
製造業において高水準の賃上げが行われている。
さらに、株価の上昇による資産効果も消費を押し

上げており、台湾の代表的な株価指数である加権
指数は、年初から 10 月初旬までに＋60％上昇し
た。これらを背景に、小売売上高は、主に生活必
需品を販売する一般店、嗜好品を多く扱う専門店ともに高水準の伸びを維持している（右下図）。
投資も拡大している。AI ブームを背景に、電子部品産業による生産能力の増強投資が活発化し
ており、同産業が台湾全体の設備投資や工場建設増加の 9 割以上を占めている。
■2026 年の実質 GDP 成長率は＋10％超へ
先行きの台湾経済を展望すると、輸出と投資を両輪とする景気拡大が続き、2026 年の実質 GDP
成長率は＋11.1％に達すると予想する。2 桁の成長率は、16 年ぶりとなる。
輸出は引き続き増加する見込みである。海外のデータセンター投資の拡大を背景に、半導体や
サーバーの輸出は好調を維持すると予想する。
投資も好調を維持する見通しである。半導体や AI サーバーのメーカーは相次いで今年の設備投
資計画を上方修正しているほか、関連部材の製造業などでも能力増強投資が拡大している。

台湾経済の主な下振れリスクは、AI 投資の収益性への懸念が高まり、AI ブームが調整局面を迎
えることである。AI サーバーに用いられる GPU は 3～6 年程度で陳腐化する可能性があり、買い
替えによる投資負担の増大が、収益性を悪化させるとの指摘もある。仮に世界の AI インフラ投資
のペースが鈍化すれば、AI 関連偏重の台湾の輸出と投資を大きく下押しすると考えられる。
また、物価高による消費下押しもリスク要因である。足元の消費者物価の前年同月比は、中央
銀行の警戒ラインである＋2％を上回って推移している。①エネルギー価格の高止まり、②パソコ
ンや携帯電話などの半導体搭載製品の価格高騰、③積極的な賃上げを背景とするサービス価格の
上昇など、足元で見られるインフレ圧力の先行きに注意が必要である。

6
フィリピン インフレは一段と加速
■物価高が景気を下押し
フィリピン景気は、外需は好調に推移している
ものの、内需の低迷を補いきれない状況が続いて
いる。
外需の面では、8 月の財輸出が前年同月比＋
27.8％と、前月の同＋10.8％から大幅に加速した
（右上図）。世界的な AI 関連需要の拡大を背景
に、電子機器・部品がけん引役となっている。輸
出が極めて好調であることから、7 月まで悪化し
ていた貿易収支にも、足元では改善の兆しがみら
れる。
これに対し、内需は依然として力強さを欠いて
いる。中東危機を背景とするエネルギー価格の高
止まりに加え、台風や豪雨による食品価格の上昇
が、家計の実質購買力を圧迫している。9 月の消
費者物価指数（CPI）は同＋7.2％と、8 月の同＋
6.1％から伸びが加速し、中銀の目標レンジ上限
である＋4％を大きく上回った（右下図）。

価格転嫁の動きは幅広い財・サービスに及んで
おり、変動の大きい食品とエネルギーを除くコア
CPI も同＋4.7％と、前月の同＋4.1％から上昇し
ている。背景には、ペソ安に伴う輸入コストの上
昇が挙げられる。フィリピン・ペソは 2 月末から本稿執筆時点までに、対ドルで▲9.2％下落した。
こうしたなか、中銀はインフレ抑制を優先し、4 月以降、3 会合連続で利上げを実施し、政策金
利は 5.0％の高水準となっている。インフレ率が目標レンジを大幅に上回るなか、今後も金融引き
締め姿勢を維持するとみられる。
■公共投資の回復に遅れ
内需の低迷は家計部門にとどまらない。昨年の洪水対策事業を巡る汚職問題を受け、政府が事
業点検や支払い審査を厳格化したため、4～6 月期の公共投資は前年に比べて大幅に減少した。足
元でもインフラ関連支出の回復は鈍く、景気の下押し要因となっている。10 月には、国会議員や
公共事業道路省職員、建設業者といった関係者の訴追が始まるなど、汚職事件を巡る捜査が本格
化している。こうした一連の動きは、中長期的に公共事業の健全化につながる一方、短期的には

工事の再開を遅らせ、投資を冷え込ませるとみられる。
先行き、堅調な AI 関連需要を背景に電子機器・部品の輸出は底堅く推移するとみられる一方、
内需は引き続き力強さを欠く見通しである。公共投資の停滞に加え、インフレ高進による実質購
買力の低下や、政策金利の高止まりによる借入コストの上昇が、個人消費や民間投資を抑制する
とみられる。
研究員 呉 子婧（wu.zijing@jri.co.jp）

JAPAN RESEARCH INSTITUTE

タ イ 輸出主導で回復も、消費に弱さ
■個人消費の回復は緩慢
タイ景気は、AI・テクノロジー関連需要の拡
大を背景に輸出と民間投資が想定を上回る一方、
個人消費は力強さを欠くなど、需要項目によっ
て勢いにばらつきが見られる。
輸出は高い伸びを維持している。8 月の財輸出
は前年同月比＋24.3％と、前月の同＋23.9％から
伸びが加速した。世界的な AI 関連需要の拡大を
受けて、電子機器・部品が輸出をけん引してい
る。民間投資も増勢を強めている。データセン
ターや電子・半導体関連設備への投資拡大が主因となっている。
これに対し、個人消費の回復は緩慢である。政府による一連の消費支援策を受け、ホテル・レ
ストランなどのサービス消費や耐久財支出は増加したものの、家計の節約志向は依然として根強
い。9 月の消費者信頼感指数は 52.1 と、中立水準の 100 を大きく下回り、本格的な改善には至っ

ていない（右上図）。背景には、GDP 比 85％を超える家計債務の返済負担が可処分所得を圧迫し
ていることに加え、中小企業向け融資の減少により雇用・所得環境の改善が遅れ、企業部門好調
の恩恵が家計に十分波及していないことがある。
■物価上昇と洪水被害が景気の下押し要因に
こうしたなか、足元では物価上昇も家計消費
の重石となっている。9 月の消費者物価指数は前
年同月比＋2.8％と、8 月の同＋2.5％から伸びが
加速した（右下図）。中東情勢の緊迫化に伴う
エネルギー価格の高止まりと、天候不順による
生鮮食品価格の上昇が主因である。
9 月以降の洪水被害の深刻化も、さらなる景気
下押し要因となっている。タイ防災減災局
（DDPM）は 10 月 4 日、洪水の影響が約 120
万世帯、326 万人以上に及んだと発表した。洪
水は農地や物流網にも被害をもたらしており、農産物の出荷停滞を通じて食品価格を一段と押し
上げる懸念がある。企業活動への影響も広がっている。タイ工業連盟（FTI）によると、工業部門

の損失額は 10 月初旬時点で約 60 億バーツに上り、とりわけ自動車産業では部品供給への懸念が
高まっている。被害が長期化すれば、部品不足や輸送コストの高騰を通じて企業の生産活動が下
押しされる可能性がある。さらに、家屋や設備の復旧に向けた政府支出の増加は、短期的には景
気を下支えする一方、中長期的な財政運営上の負担となる可能性もある。
以上を踏まえると、需要面と供給面の両方からの下押し圧力が強まることで、先行きの景気は
力強さを欠く展開が続く見通しである。2026 年の実質 GDP 成長率は＋1.8％と、前年の＋2.4％
から減速すると予想する。

中 国 景気減速にようやく歯止め
■7～9 月期の成長率は持ち直し
中国景気は減速に歯止めがかかった。本年 7～
9 月期の実質 GDP は前年同期比＋4.5％と、前期
から若干持ち直した（右上図）。
押し上げの主因は、好調な輸出である。7～9
月期のドル建て輸出額は同＋28.4％と、4～6 月
期（同＋20.1％）に比べて伸びが加速した。地域
別では、ASEAN や EU 向けが好調に推移した。
米国向けも、両国の経済関係の改善の流れを受け、
前年同月比プラスが続いている。品目別では、
EV を含む自動車、AI ブームを背景とした IT 関連財がけん引した。
一方、内需は引き続き低迷している。固定資産投資は、過剰生産や不動産不況といった構造的
な下押し要因が残存し、総じて低調であった。とくに、不動産開発投資は前年同期比でマイナス
幅が拡大している。政府が製造業高度化の象徴として重視するハイテク製造業など、一部の業種

では堅調に拡大したものの、投資全体を押し上げるには至っていない。
消費は、財を中心に弱含んでいる。7～9 月期の小売売上高は同＋0.7％と、前期からほぼ横ばい
で推移した。背景には、①自動車が買い替え補助金による需要の反動減と、原油高によるガソリ
ン車需要の減退から大幅減となったこと、②不動産不況を受け、家具・建材などの売上の不振が
続いていること、の 2 点がある。旅行や外食は、9 月末の大型連休期間中は需要が増加したものの、
一過性の持ち直しに終わった。
■先行き、景気刺激策が押し上げ要因に
こうした内需の低迷を受け、政府は 9 月末以降、
追加対策を発表している（右下表）。景気浮揚効
果の観点で注目される措置としては、まず住宅ロ
ーンの利子補給が挙げられる。また、金融緩和を
通じて、ハイテク分野への投資や次世代通信網・
物流網の整備といった「六つのネットワーク」を
推進しようとしている。これらの取り組みは、住
宅関連需要の持ち直しや、ハイテク製造業とイン
フラ部門の投資拡大などをもたらし、内需を押し

上げると見込まれる。加えて、外需も当面は好調
を維持するため、10～12 月期の成長率は 7～9
月期を若干上回る見通しである。その結果、2026
年通年では政府目標（＋4.5～5.0％）をかろうじ
て達成できる見込みである。
もっとも、それを踏まえても内需は自律的な回復には至らないとみられ、政策効果が薄れる 2027
年入り後に、景気は再び減速すると予想される。
主任研究員 佐野 淳也（sano.junya@jri.co.jp）

Document 485: インドネシア中銀はルピア安阻止へ利上げも、政策運営の難しさは増す一方 ～政府の景気下支え要請と金融市場の通貨安定要求との_bb4069fc

Source PDF: all report pdf/インドネシア中銀はルピア安阻止へ利上げも、政策運営の難しさは増す一方 ～政府の景気下支え要請と金融市場の通貨安定要求との_bb4069fc.pdf
Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-11-18

### Page 1

Asia Trends

2026年11月 18日

1/4 
インドネシア中銀はルピア安阻止へ利上げも、政策運営の難しさは増す一方 
～政府の景気下支え要請と金融市場の通貨安定要求との「板挟み」が続く可能性～ 
 経済調査部 主席エコノミスト  西濵 徹 
（℡：050-5474-7495）

（要旨） 
○ インドネシア銀行（中銀）は 11月 17～18日に開催した定例の月例理事会において、政策金
利を 25bp引き上げて 6.00％とすることを決定した。５会合ぶりとなる利上げの背景には、
MSCIによる同国株式市場の格下げ懸念やギブラン副大統領の辞任表明などを受けた金融市
場の不安定化がある。 
○ 中銀は今回の決定を、ルピア相場の安定と物価目標の達成に向けた「先制的措置」と説明し、
ＦＲＢによる追加利上げの可能性も念頭に置いた対応であることを明らかにした。一方で、
金融引き締めによる景気への悪影響を抑えるべく、緩和的なマクロプルーデンス政策や流動
性供給策を通じて経済活動を下支えする方針も示した。 
○ 近年の法改正によって中銀には経済成長や雇用創出への貢献が一段と求められるとともに、
政治介入への懸念も強まっている。先行きも、米国の金融政策やルピア相場の動向に左右さ
れるなか、中銀は政府からの景気下支え要請と金融市場からの通貨安定要求との「板挟み」
に置かれ、難しい政策運営を迫られる展開が続くと予想される。

【インドネシア中銀、ルピア相場の安定と物価安定への「先制的措置」として利上げを決定】 
インドネシア銀行（中銀）は、11月 17～18日に開催した月例理事会において、政策金利である
ＢＩレートを 25bp引き上げ、6.00％とすることを決定した。なお、10月の前回会合では、政策金
利を据え置く一方、金利コリドーの下限である翌日物預金ファシリティ金利を 25bp 引き上げて
5.00％とする決定を行った。これには、ルピア建て短期資金の保有を促すとともに、通貨安定に向
けて海外からの資金流入を促進する狙いがあったとみられる。さらに、景気下支えと国内の流動性
確保に向けて、預金準備率の引き下げを決定した。加えて、為替ヘッジ取引に対する優遇措置を強
化し、海外からの資金流入を促す方針も示した（注１）。こうしたことから、事前の市場予想では、
中銀が今回会合でも様子見姿勢を維持するとの見方が優勢であった。しかし、中銀はこうした見方

注１ 10月21日付レポート「インドネシア中銀､市場動揺も､政策運営は政府と市場の｢板挟み｣が続く」 
  （https://www.dlri.co.jp/report/macro/666784.html）

### Page 2

2/4 
に反して、５会合ぶりの利上げに動いた。 
背景には、足元で同国金融市場が混乱に直面していることがある。きっかけは、指数算出会社の
MSCIが 10月、同国市場を「スタンドアローン市場」に格下げする案を示し、市場参加者との協議
を開始したことにある（注２）。 MSCIは 2027年４月末まで市場参加者から意見を募るとともに、同
年６月末までに最終判断を示すとしている。協議を通じて市場のガバナンス機能の実質的な改善を
裏付ける明確な証拠が確認されなければ、格下げが実施される見通しである。この発表を受けて、
主要株価指数（ジャカルタ総合指数）は一段と下振れし、ルピア相場にも下押し圧力がかかった。
さらに、今月 10日にはギブラン副大統領
が辞任の意向を表明し（注３）、12日に議
会に辞表を提出した。ギブラン氏の辞任
により、同氏の父であるジョコ前大統領
とプラボウォ大統領との政治的関係に影
響が及ぶとの見方があったものの、直後
にジョコ氏はプラボウォ氏を支持する意
向を明らかにした。このため、金融市場
では政治的混乱の拡大を警戒する動きが
一段と強まる事態には至っていないもの
の（図１）、今後の政局の行方については
予断を許さない状況が続いている。

【中銀は景気に配慮も、ルピア相場の最安値更新を受けて、相場安定へ利上げに舵を切る】 
会合後に公表した声明文では、今回の決定について「世界的な不確実性が高まるなか、ルピア相
場の安定と 2026年および 2027年の物価目標の達成を目指すための先制的な措置」と説明した。先
行きについては、「ルピア相場の安定強化を通じてインフレを目標レンジ内に収めるべく、一貫し
た金融政策を行う」一方、「緩和的なマクロプルーデンス政策を強化し、金融システムの安定を維持
しつつ、与信・融資の拡大を通じて実体経済を下支えする」として、景気に配慮する姿勢を示した。 
背景には、中銀の政策運営において、景気動向への配慮がこれまで以上に求められるようになっ
ていることがある。2023年に成立した金融部門発展強化法（P2SK法）では、中銀の政策目的に持
続可能な経済成長への支援が位置付けられた。さらに、大統領が危機的事態を宣言した場合には、
中銀による政府からの国債直接購入（財政ファイナンス）も認められた。今年６月に成立した改正
P2SK法では、中銀の政策運営において、経済成長や雇用創出に資することが一段と明確化された。

注２ 10月16日付レポート「インドネシア株に逆風､MSCIが｢スタンドアローン｣への格下げ検討」 
  （https://www.dlri.co.jp/report/macro/666610.html） 
注３ 11月11日付レポート「インドネシア､ギブラン副大統領の辞任意向表明で政局に新たな波乱」 
  （https://www.dlri.co.jp/report/macro/678159.html）

3/4 
加えて、国会が独立機関である金融規制当局（金融サービス庁（ＯＪＫ））や中銀などの業績を評価
し、勧告を行う仕組みも導入されるなど、政治介入への懸念も強まっている。こうした状況にもか
かわらず、今回はルピア相場が最安値近傍で推移するなか、利上げを通じた相場安定を優先する姿
勢を強めたと捉えられる。 
世界経済については、「不確実性が高止まりしている」ほか、「金融市場のボラティリティが高ま
っている」との認識を示した。しかし、2026年の世界経済の成長率については「約 3.1％」と従来
見通し（３％）からわずかに上方修正した。一方、同国経済については「世界経済が低調に推移す
るなかでも堅調」としつつ、「今後はさらなる成長の底上げが必要」との見方を示したほか、「2026
年の経済成長率は＋4.9～5.7％になる」との従来見通しを据え置いた。対外収支についても「世界
的な不確実性が高止まりするなか、国際
収支を強化する必要がある」としつつ、
「10月末時点の外貨準備高は輸入の 5.3
ヵ月分に相当する」と説明する一方、2026
年の経常赤字見通しへの言及はなかっ
た。ルピア相場については、「為替介入や
ヘッジ取引への優遇措置などを通じて相
場の安定強化を図っている」として、従来
からの対策を強化する考えを示した。物
価動向については、「政府が設定した目標
の範囲内で維持されている」（図２）とし
たほか、足元では「天候不順による食料インフレの懸念が高まっている」ものの、「物価を目標レン
ジ内に収めるべく、為替相場の安定に取り組むとともに、政府も食料インフレ対策を強化する」と
の見方を示した。

【中銀首脳は相場安定を重視する姿勢を強調も、政府と金融市場との「板挟み」が続く可能性は高い】 
会合後に記者会見に臨んだ中銀のデストリ総裁は、今回の利上げ決定について「ルピア相場の安
定化策の強化を目指したもの」としたうえで、「インフレを目標レンジに収めることを目的とした
先制的な取り組み」であるとともに、「ＦＲＢ（米連邦準備制度理事会）による利上げ実施の可能性
を見据えた先制的な取り組み」と説明した。先行きについても、「ルピア相場の安定に向けて一貫し
た取り組みを進める」との考えを示した。そのうえで、「通貨間のレポ取引を通じて銀行部門の流動
性を支援すべく、為替ヘッジに対するインセンティブをさらに高める」とともに、「2027年３月ま
でに通貨間レポ取引を開始できるよう準備を進めている」と説明した。そして、利上げ実施に際し
ては「流動性が過度にひっ迫しないよう、他の政策を組み合わせて実施する」として、景気にも配
慮する考えを滲ませた。 
同席したアイダ上級副総裁は、ＦＲＢによる利上げについて「基本シナリオでは、年内に１回実
施され、来年は据え置かれる」との見通しを示す一方、「状況を注視する」とした。そして、先行き

りません。
4/4
の政策運営については「物価や実体経済のデータに応じて決定される」と述べるにとどめた。前回
会合における翌日物預金ファシリティ金利の引き上げについては「金融政策の正常化に向けた取り
組みの一環」としたうえで、今回の決定については「世界情勢を踏まえたもの」と説明した。また、
為替介入については「年間を通じて実施してきた」と述べた。
一連の発言の背景には、前述の通り、中銀に対して経済成長や雇用創出を支援する役割も求めら
れるようになっていることがある。政治介入が懸念される動きもみられるなか、中銀の政策運営は
これまで以上に難しさを増している。先行きも、中銀は政府からの景気下支え要請と、金融市場か
らの通貨安定要求との「板挟み」に置かれ、難しい政策運営を迫られる局面が続くと予想される。
